<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **s-monotonicity** [claim](#stmt-lt-monotonicity) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-monotonicity): Test stationarity with a radial field and pass to the limit at integer-density points. -> lower mass bound and mean-value inequality
- **s-curved-variation** [claim](#stmt-lt-curvedvariation) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-curvedvariation): Lift the tangent field and compute its derivative blocks. -> curved horizontal first variation
- **s-jacobian** [claim](#stmt-lt-jacobian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-jacobian): Projection Jacobian expansion and the area formula. -> integer count and Jacobian bounds
- **s-potential** [claim](#stmt-lt-potential) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-potential): Calderon-Zygmund decomposition plus a harmonic remainder. -> weak L1 and distributional control
- **s-scalar** [claim](#stmt-lt-scalar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-scalar): Apply the potential estimate to the projected mass and use integrality. -> unique integer and mass control
- **s-config** [claim](#stmt-lt-config) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-config): Quantile selection, joint counts and dyadic stopping. -> configuration with moment and support bounds
- **s-weighted** [claim](#stmt-lt-weightedballs) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-weightedballs): Cover by comparable balls and sum with Gaussian weights. -> Gaussian-weighted estimates
- **s-gaussian** [claim](#stmt-lt-gaussian) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-gaussian): Differentiate the Gaussian kernel and use the horizontal variation. -> mass identity in scale
- **s-height-tests** [claim](#stmt-lt-heighttests) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lt-heighttests): Differentiate height-dependent ambient fields and estimate frame errors. -> height identities
- **s-contact** [claim](#stmt-ct-contact) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ct-contact): Fixed-point selection of self-maximizing heat potentials and a maximum principle. -> contact measure with signs
- **s-localization** [claim](#stmt-ct-localization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ct-localization): Commutator estimate for the weight and a Gaussian far-field bound. -> localized sign conditions
- **s-separated** [claim](#stmt-ct-separated) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ct-separated): Recursion over balls with lower-integer stationary pieces and random thresholds. -> separated-configuration bound
- **s-bottom** [claim](#stmt-ex-bottom) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-bottom): Grid-cube averaging, differentiation of measures and the Jacobian expansion. -> vanishing bottom mass
- **s-envelope** [claim](#stmt-ex-envelope) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-envelope): Maximizing-scale argument using the cumulative sign. -> future envelope
- **s-initialization** [claim](#stmt-ex-initialization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-initialization): Choose wells from a configuration witness and compare moments. -> initial barrier
- **s-barrier** [claim](#stmt-ex-barrier) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-barrier): Differential comparison with merger rule and square completion. -> barrier along the path
- **s-bands** [claim](#stmt-ex-bands) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ex-bands): Pigeonhole over merger events using inverse rate comparisons. -> merger-free band traversal
- **s-witnesses** [claim](#stmt-pk-witnesses) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pk-witnesses): Time-bin estimate and a differential contradiction on the cumulative bank. -> ordered witness scales
- **s-reverse** [claim](#stmt-pk-reverse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pk-reverse): Normal flux lower bound with Hoelder control of high-density parts. -> controlled-density cube
- **s-packing** [claim](#stmt-pk-packing) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pk-packing): Stopping-time retracing and paying generations. -> packing bound
- **s-contact-charge** [claim](#stmt-pk-contact-charge) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pk-contact-charge): Blocking-ball selection and volume comparison. -> outer mass bound
- **s-signed** [claim](#stmt-sx-signed) / [proof](#proof-stmt-pk-contact-charge-2): Induction on Q: contact charging against the packed witness set. -> signed-excess estimate
- **s-fit** [claim](#stmt-ce-fit) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ce-fit): Jacobi right inverse, contraction for the nonlinear correction, elliptic comparison. -> minimal fits
- **s-failed** [claim](#stmt-ce-failed) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ce-failed): Normalized limits, signed estimate and unique continuation by frequency. -> separation from a failed fit
- **s-center** [claim](#stmt-ce-center) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ce-center): Partition-of-unity gluing and a defect-charging covering argument. -> center and influence region
- **s-weights** [claim](#stmt-ce-weights) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ce-weights): Witness failures and inward subballs against polynomial weights. -> weighted height bounds
- **s-constancy** [claim](#stmt-ce-constancy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ce-constancy): Constancy of multiplicity from tangential first variation. -> regularity when height vanishes
- **s-collar** [claim](#stmt-in-collar) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-collar): Bulk cells with the signed estimate and a radial divergence equation on the thin part. -> collar comparison
- **s-frequency** [claim](#stmt-in-frequency) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-frequency): Test normal and horizontal stationarity and differentiate the height. -> frequency inequalities
- **s-bootstrap** [claim](#stmt-in-bootstrap) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-bootstrap): Differential inequalities for the frequency ratio and integration. -> bounded frequency and doubling
- **s-finish** [claim](#stmt-in-finish) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-finish): Higher height moment against vanishing influence mass gives a contradiction. -> typical points are regular
- **s-main** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-finish-2): Typical points have full measure and monotonicity transfers null sets to Hausdorff measure. -> singular set is null
- **s-round-spheres** [claim](#stmt-cor-round-spheres) / [proof](#proof-stmt-in-finish-3): Cone over the sphere varifold, main theorem one dimension up, then slicing. -> spherical conclusion

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-singular-set-null**: For every stationary integral varifold in an open Euclidean set, with arbitrary positive dimension and codimension, the singular set has zero measure in the dimension of the varifold.
- **inv-round-sphere-singular-null**: The same measure-zero conclusion holds for an integral varifold on a round sphere that is stationary for variations tangent to the sphere, on spheres of every positive radius.
- **inv-monotonicity-lower-mass**: At every support point of a stationary integral varifold the mass of a ball whose closure lies in the domain is bounded below by the Euclidean volume factor times the radius to the dimension.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-non-integer-density**: Apply the almost-everywhere regularity conclusion to a varifold with real-valued, non-integer density. -> The statement is proved only for integer multiplicity; the integer count is used to select a unique integer on balls and to build configurations of heights, so the conclusion is not available for generalized densities.
- **anti-non-stationary**: Use the measure-zero singular set result for a varifold that is not stationary, for instance one with nonzero generalized mean curvature. -> Stationarity supplies monotonicity, the first-variation identities and the signed-excess comparisons; without it none of these tools holds and singular sets of positive measure are not excluded by the argument.
- **anti-rectifiability-as-regularity**: Read smooth rectifiability of the varifold as already giving a smooth minimal neighborhood at almost every point. -> A countable smooth covering outside a null set does not give equality with one smooth minimal submanifold on a whole neighborhood; that stronger statement is what the theorem adds.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `not_applicable`. The paper proves a qualitative regularity theorem (the singular set has zero Hausdorff measure) by induction, compactness and contradiction arguments; it states no finite procedure, explicit construction or computable bound that a program could output, so there is nothing for a reference implementation or independent oracle to decide.

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The result is an existential regularity statement: the singular set has zero measure. It supplies no procedure, interface or data format that a program could implement. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The theorem concerns stationary integral varifolds in the continuum. It provides no discretisation, time stepping or error control that a physics or graphics engine could use. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper is a pure regularity theory. It offers no checkable certificate or measurable quantity from which a diagnostic tool could be built. |
| algorithms | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The proof is an induction built on contradiction and compactness arguments, with no finite algorithm and no stated complexity. It is a non-constructive existence result, so no pseudocode is warranted. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No container, index or representation is introduced. The dyadic cubes and configurations in the proof are analytic bookkeeping devices rather than data structures with operations. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no computation, dataflow or arithmetic kernel in the paper to map to hardware. The estimates are analytic and carry no cost model. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns hardness assumptions, adversaries or randomness for security. The random thresholds in the proof are an averaging device and not a source of secrecy. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no learning setup, loss or generalisation claim. The statement concerns stationary varifolds and gives no guarantee about fitted or trained models. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The varifolds are stationary points of area, not solutions of a stated optimisation problem. The result gives no objective, feasible set or convergence rate, and requires no minimizing property. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper has no processes, communication or consensus. The localisation and covering arguments are measure-theoretic and have no concurrency content. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The value is theoretical: it settles an almost-everywhere regularity conjecture for stationary integral varifolds in Euclidean sets and on round spheres. It does not translate to an engineering construction. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-integer-multiplicity** (hypothesis): The weight function must take positive integer values. Integrality is used to select a unique integer count on balls, to propagate it across overlapping balls and to build configurations of heights, so the argument does not carry over to real-valued densities. Blocked use: ['Applying the measure-zero singular set conclusion to a varifold with non-integer density', 'Reusing the integer-count selection step for generalized varifolds']
- **limit-stationarity** (hypothesis): Stationarity is the standing hypothesis. Monotonicity, the first-variation identities and the signed-excess comparisons all rest on it, and without it the argument gives no control on the singular set. Blocked use: ['Using the theorem for a varifold with nonzero generalized mean curvature', 'Using the monotonicity lower mass bound at support points of a non-stationary varifold']
- **limit-measure-zero-only** (formal_scope): The conclusion is that the singular set is null for the measure in the dimension of the varifold. The paper states no quantitative size, dimension bound or structure for the singular set beyond this. Blocked use: ['Quoting a Hausdorff dimension bound for the singular set from this paper', 'Inferring that the singular set is empty, discrete or a submanifold']
- **limit-no-extra-hypotheses-scope** (formal_scope): The main theorem needs no stability, minimizing, orientability, density bound or codimension-one condition. This is a statement about the hypotheses of the theorem only and does not extend to any other regularity conclusion. Blocked use: ['Reading the absence of extra hypotheses as a regularity claim for non-integral or non-stationary objects']
- **limit-ambient-geometry** (formal_scope): Only open subsets of Euclidean space and round spheres are treated. Stationarity on a sphere means stationarity for variations tangent to the sphere, and the paper does not state the conclusion for general ambient Riemannian metrics. Blocked use: ['Applying the theorem to a varifold in a general Riemannian manifold', 'Using the sphere corollary without tangential stationarity']
- **limit-sphere-radius-scope** (formal_scope): The spherical conclusion is stated for every round sphere of positive radius, obtained by a cone reduction to the Euclidean theorem in one dimension higher. It is not claimed for non-round metrics. Blocked use: ['Transferring the sphere corollary to ellipsoids or other non-round metrics']
- **limit-signed-excess-regime** (hypothesis): The signed-excess estimate holds for a fixed positive integer count and a positive exponent, once the total excess is small and the large threshold is exceeded. Outside the smallness regime of the theorem there is no stated gain. Blocked use: ['Using the signed-excess bound when the excess on the working ball is not small', 'Using the bound with constants that vary with the scale']
- **limit-fit-derivative-order** (hypothesis): The minimal-fit construction needs a finite derivative order at least a fixed threshold, and its constants depend on that order and the dimensions. It is not asserted for lower regularity of the reference graph. Blocked use: ['Running the fit and gluing steps with reference graphs of low regularity']
- **limit-constants-dependence** (approximation): All estimates are interior and local, and constants depend on the dimensions, an upper bound for the integer count and the fixed coordinate geometry. The paper gives no explicit values, so none can be read off for a given varifold. Blocked use: ['Quoting numerical radii or thresholds for a specific varifold from the proof', 'Treating the constants as uniform over varying ambient geometry']
- **limit-scalar-enlargement** (edge_case): The scalar mass estimate needs a fixed interior enlargement and a specified integer. It is not an estimate on a ball without room or with an unspecified count. Blocked use: ['Using the mass control on a ball touching the boundary of the working region', 'Applying it before the integer count has been identified']
- **limit-contact-sign-scope** (nonconstructive): The contact measure is produced by a fixed-point and weak-limit argument and gives a pointwise sign only at the contact scale. At larger scales only the cumulative integral is signed, and no explicit contact set is provided. Blocked use: ['Assuming a pointwise sign of the source at scales above the contact scale', 'Computing the contact measure explicitly from the construction']
- **limit-typical-point-scope** (edge_case): The vanishing-height result is proved at points satisfying the typical-point hypotheses, namely a tangent multiplicity plane and a defect set of relative density zero. Extension to every support point is made only afterwards, by measure-theoretic covering from the null conclusion. Blocked use: ['Claiming regularity at a prescribed support point not known to be typical']
- **limit-single-sheet-degenerate** (edge_case): When the integer count is one, the separated-group and multiple-well parts are vacuous, so no failure of a fit occurs. Conclusions about separation from a failed fit say nothing in that case. Blocked use: ['Looking for separated sheets of lower multiplicity when the count is one']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We resolve the almost-everywhere regularity conjecture of Brena, Decio, and De Lellis for stationary integral varifolds of arbitrary positive dimension and codimension in Euclidean open sets. The singular set has zero measure in the dimension of the varifold: near almost every support point, the varifold is a constant positive integer multiple of a smooth embedded minimal submanifold. The corresponding statement also holds on round spheres.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

A stationary integral varifold is a measure-theoretic minimal surface with integer multiplicity. Its tangent plane exists almost everywhere, but a tangent plane alone need not describe an entire neighborhood. The basic regularity question is whether the points without a smooth minimal neighborhood can carry positive measure. We prove that they have zero $m$-dimensional measure.

Let $m,n\geq1$, let $U\subset\mathbb R^{m+n}$ be open, and let $E\subset U$ be countably $m$-rectifiable. Let $\theta:E\to\mathbb N\setminus\{0\}$ be measurable and locally integrable with respect to $\mathcal H^m$. Write $$\mu(A)=\int_{E\cap A}\theta\,\mathop{}\!d\mathcal H^m,
 \qquad P(x)=P_{T_xE}.$$ The associated integral varifold $V$ is stationary if <a id="eq:stationarity"></a>$$\label{eq:stationarity}
 \int \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P(x)DX(x))\,\mathop{}\!d\mu(x)=0
 \quad\text{for every }X\in C_c^1(U;\mathbb R^{m+n}).$$ A point $p\in\mathop{\mathrm{spt}}\mu\cap U$ is *regular* if there are a neighborhood $W$ of $p$, a smooth embedded minimal $m$-submanifold $\Sigma\subset W$, and an integer $q\geq1$ such that the restriction of the varifold to $W$ is $q|\Sigma|$. The remaining support points form $\operatorname{Sing}V$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Every stationary integral $m$-varifold in an open subset of $\mathbb R^{m+n}$ satisfies $$\mathcal H^m(\operatorname{Sing}V)=0.$$ Here $m,n\geq1$ are arbitrary. No stability, minimizing, orientability, density upper bound, or codimension-one hypothesis is required.*

</div>

<a id="stmt-cor-round-spheres"></a>
<div id="cor:round-spheres" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2** (Round spheres). *Let $m,n\geq1$ and let $V$ be an integral $m$-varifold on the full unit round sphere $S^{m+n}$, stationary for variations tangent to the sphere. A support point is regular if on a spherical neighborhood $V=q|\Sigma|$ for an integer $q\geq1$ and a smooth embedded minimal $m$-submanifold $\Sigma$ of the sphere. If $\operatorname{Sing}_S V$ denotes the remaining support points, then $$\mathcal H^m_{g_{\mathrm{round}}}(\operatorname{Sing}_S V)=0.$$ No fixed upper bound on mass, density, or multiplicity is required, nor are stability, orientability, or mod-two cyclicity. The same conclusion holds on every round sphere of positive radius.*

</div>

derives this corollary from [thm:main](#thm:main) by a separate cone argument. That proof adapts the hypersurface first-variation calculation in (OpenAI 2026, Lemma 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[OpenAISolomonYau2026](#ref-OpenAISolomonYau2026)] and rederives it in the present dimensions.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Background and significance

Allard’s regularity theory gives a relatively open dense regular set and, in particular, regularity at density-one points (Allard 1972) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Allard1972](#ref-Allard1972)]; see also (Allard 1987, Theorem 23) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Allard1987](#ref-Allard1987)] and (Simon 2018, chap. 5, Theorem 6.1 and Corollary 6.3) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)]. Higher integer density presents a different issue: dividing a varifold by its density at a point need not preserve the almost-everywhere lower density bound required by that theorem in a neighborhood. Standard accounts of the first variation, monotonicity, and integral varifolds may be found in (Simon 1983) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[Simon1983](#ref-Simon1983)].

Simon’s 2018 notes already record the question whether the singular set must be $\mathcal H^m$-null even when the generalized mean curvature is zero (Simon 2018, chap. 5, Remark 6.4) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)]. The measure-zero singular-set assertion is Conjecture 1.2 of Brena, Decio, and De Lellis (Brena, Decio, and De Lellis 2025) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[BDD2025](#ref-BDD2025)]. proves this conjecture in its full Euclidean scope. They isolate a local criterion in Conjecture 1.3: a support point with a unique tangent varifold $Q|\Pi|$, where $\Pi$ is an $m$-plane and $Q$ a positive integer, should be regular when the mass of $\{\theta\ne Q\}$ in a ball of radius $r$ about it is $o(r^m)$. They then propose approximating the varifold by a smooth minimal graph with the squared-height integral vanishing to every order, followed by a unique-continuation step that upgrades this contact to local support containment (Brena, Decio, and De Lellis 2025, Conjectures 1.3–1.5) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[BDD2025](#ref-BDD2025)]. Their analysis of multiplicity and height in flat cylinders also provides useful precedents for the local estimates developed here (Brena, Decio, and De Lellis 2025, Theorem 1.9 and Lemma 2.3) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[BDD2025](#ref-BDD2025)].

Related progress concerns the differentiability of a countable covering. Menne proved second-order rectifiability for integral varifolds of locally bounded first variation (Menne 2013, Theorem 3.6) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Menne2013](#ref-Menne2013)]. For stationary integral varifolds of dimension at least two, Brena, De Lellis, and Franceschini proved smooth rectifiability and infinite-order approximation at $\mu$-almost every point by one smooth graph (Brena, De Lellis, and Franceschini 2025, Theorems 1.2 and 1.3) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[BDLF2025](#ref-BDLF2025)]. The approximating graph in the latter statement is not asserted to be minimal; Brena–Decio–De Lellis present this as a weaker form of their approximation conjecture (Brena, Decio, and De Lellis 2025, Theorem 1.8) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BDD2025](#ref-BDD2025)]. Kolasiński obtains rectifiability of every finite order and an alternative proof of the smooth-rectifiability conclusion, following an architecture he credits to Menne (Kolasiński 2026, Theorem 1.1, Corollary 10.8, and the historical note in Section 1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Kolasinski2026](#ref-Kolasinski2026)]. Smooth rectifiability gives a countable smooth covering outside a $\mu$-null set; [thm:main](#thm:main) requires equality with one smooth minimal submanifold throughout a neighborhood of almost every point.

In codimension one, Hirsch and Spolaor prove almost-everywhere regularity for integral currents that are stationary as varifolds and have density at most two at every support point (Hirsch and Spolaor 2025, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[HS2025](#ref-HS2025)]. These current and multiplicity hypotheses distinguish that result from the arbitrary-codimension statement here.

De Lellis, Hirsch, Lihn, and Spolaor construct, in a smooth Riemannian metric, a stationary varifold with a double-plane tangent at an exceptional point and topology accumulating there (De Lellis et al. 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[DLHLS2026](#ref-DLHLS2026)].[^1]

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The argument

<a id="eq:intro-signed"></a>
The main estimate compares projected mass and tilt with their leading terms kept signed. In product coordinates $\mathbb R^m\times\mathbb R^n$, let $\pi$ be projection onto the first factor, fix a positive integer $Q$, and let $P_{vv}$ be the vertical block of the tangent projection. With lengths and mass normalized to the working radius, define $$M=\pi_\#\mu-Q\,\mathop{}\!dy,
 \qquad D=\pi_\#(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}P_{vv}\,\mu).$$ Here $|M|$ denotes the total variation of the signed measure $M$, and $\tau=|M|+D$ is the positive excess measure in this flat setting. When $\tau$ has mass at most $d$ on a fixed enlarged ball, we prove an interior bound of the form $$\label{eq:intro-signed}
 \int w(2M-D)
 \leq d\exp\!\left[-F_Q\!\left(\log\frac1d\right)\right]$$ for nonnegative Lipschitz weights with fixed bounds. The explicit function $F_Q$ grows faster than every fixed power of $\log T$ as $T\to\infty$. [sx:signed](#sx:signed) gives the precise statement, including the curvature error for a reference surface of sufficiently high finite regularity. Retaining the sign permits a substantially smaller error than an absolute-value estimate.

The proof of <a href="#eq:intro-signed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:intro-signed">[eq:intro-signed]</a> is an induction on $Q$. A heat-potential construction turns a positive violation into a positive measure on selected centers and scales. At each selected point it gives an initial sign and a nonnegative cumulative integral along larger scales; the selection includes vertical sides of the stopping boundary. For configurations whose normal heights separate into groups, a stopping argument reduces the estimate to stationary restrictions with smaller integer counts. The resulting comparison controls tilt away from the groups.

Along a selected path, we take a supremum of damped signed-mass averages over larger scales and compare it with a clipped squared-distance moment from an unordered list of at most $Q$ representative normal heights. A weighted combination of this moment and the cumulative contact integral stays below the supremum even when representative heights merge. On an interval without a merger, this comparison forces a quantitative amount of tilt at many scales. Normal stationarity turns that tilt into a flux pairing height displacement with radial tilt. A higher height moment prevents all of that flux from concentrating on sets that have small base volume but large local excess density. At each selected scale this yields a base cube in which a quantitative fraction of the $\tau$-mass remains below a density threshold at all smaller dyadic scales. Packing these nested cubes shows that the union of their full innermost members has small $\tau$-mass. A final charge bounds the contact measure by the mass of that union, contradicting the original violation.

For regularity, fix a point where dilated varifolds converge to a multiplicity-$Q$ plane and the mass of other multiplicities has relative density zero. At each nearby base point, we fit minimal graphs at successive scales, stopping when a prescribed bound on the support height fails. The correction of each fit is closely related to the Jacobi regularization and nonlinear correction of Brena–De Lellis–Franceschini (Brena, De Lellis, and Franceschini 2025, sec. 2 and 6) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[BDLF2025](#ref-BDLF2025)]. A centered first-moment estimate, combined with elliptic regularity, makes neighboring fits agree to second order in their height error. The signed estimate is first used in a contradiction argument for a sequence of failed fits normalized by their height errors. Together with the limiting first-variation identities, its one-sided inequality shows that the limiting squared-height measure is nonzero in every interior subball. Strong control of the centered first moment, together with the integer count $Q$, then forces that second moment to come from separated projection entries of lower multiplicity on a definite portion of each such ball. The regions influenced by failures therefore have vanishing relative volume.

The compatible fits can now be glued into one fixed center of sufficiently high finite regularity. The weighted integral of the squared mean curvature and the square of its first derivative is bounded by the corresponding squared-height integral. The support height is zero outside the regions influenced by failures. Almgren developed the center-manifold approach to higher-multiplicity regularity (Almgren 2000) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Almgren2000](#ref-Almgren2000)]; De Lellis–Spadaro construct their center by gluing smoothed local averages (De Lellis and Spadaro 2016a) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[DLS2016II](#ref-DLS2016II)]. For the inward step we use the comparison of height and energy in the frequency method of this program (De Lellis and Spadaro 2011, sec. 3.4) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[DLS2011](#ref-DLS2011)], including its cutoff form on a center manifold (De Lellis and Spadaro 2016b, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[DLS2016III](#ref-DLS2016III)]. The minimizing-current and Dirichlet-minimizing hypotheses in the Almgren–De Lellis–Spadaro regularity results are unavailable here, so the needed inequalities are derived from stationarity and the signed estimate.

This second use of signed excess takes place at finite radii relative to the fixed center. Interior balls retain the leading signed comparison between projected mass and tilt. A separate radial test controls the thin outer collar by a small multiple of the tilt in the radial direction. It thereby preserves the leading tilt coefficient when the comparison is inserted into the frequency identities. The resulting inward comparison supplies height doubling without assuming it initially. Along a sequence of radii with controlled frequency, the local mass estimate transfers the vanishing relative volume of the failure regions to vanishing relative projected mass. A higher height moment then excludes nonzero height carried by those regions. The support lies on the center in a neighborhood, and stationarity gives constant multiplicity and makes the center a smooth minimal graph.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### Organization.

[sec:local](#sec:local) establishes the first-variation, integer-count, and height estimates. [sec:signed-statement](#sec:signed-statement) states the signed-excess theorem and fixes its parameter hierarchy. [sec:contact](#sec:contact) constructs the heat contacts and reduces separated configurations to lower multiplicities; [sec:excess](#sec:excess) develops the outward comparison and controls mergers; [sec:packing](#sec:packing) closes the integer induction by packing witness scales. [sec:centering](#sec:centering) constructs the center from minimal fits and multiplicity defects. [sec:inward](#sec:inward) derives inward doubling, forces the height to vanish, and proves the Hausdorff-measure conclusion on the entire support. [sec:spheres](#sec:spheres) proves the round-sphere consequence by the separate cone argument.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Conventions

All estimates are interior and local. Constants may depend on $m,n$, an upper bound for the particular integer count $Q$, and the fixed coordinate geometry. They are independent of the small scale and excess unless stated otherwise. Fixed changes of concentric radii are allowed when positive interior room is retained. A dot denotes differentiation with respect to the logarithm of the radius. Every projection count includes the integer multiplicity. We distinguish a positive tensor from the measure obtained by multiplying it by $\mu$; projections of the latter to the base are stated when introduced. [sec:signed-statement](#sec:signed-statement) specifies the quantitative hierarchy used in the induction.

<a id="sec:local"></a>
### Local first variation and height estimates

We first collect the local estimates used in the signed comparison. Their constants depend on the dimensions, an upper bound for the integer under consideration, fixed interior enlargement factors, and the indicated bounds for the coordinates. Counts in a normal fiber always include integer multiplicity. A configuration of size $Q$ is therefore an unordered list of $Q$ vectors, with repetitions allowed.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### First variation and integer counts

We begin with the standard stationary monotonicity and mean-value arguments; compare (Allard 1987, Theorem 9) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Allard1987](#ref-Allard1987)] for monotonicity. We include the derivation to retain the lower mass bound at every point of the support.

<a id="stmt-lt-monotonicity"></a>
<div id="lt:monotonicity" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lt-lower-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-mean-value"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Monotonicity and mean value). *Let $V$ be stationary and integral in an open Euclidean set. If $p\in\mathop{\mathrm{spt}}\mu$ and $\overline{\mathbf B_r(p)}$ lies in that set, then $$\label{eq:lt-lower-mass}
 \mu(\mathbf B_r(p))\ge\omega_m r^m.$$ If $u\ge0$ is a $C^2$ ambient function with $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(P_TD^2u)\ge0$ for $V$-almost every $(x,T)$ in the ball, then $$\label{eq:lt-mean-value}
 u(p)\le \frac{1}{\omega_m r^m}
                  \int_{\mathbf B_r(p)}u\,\mathop{}\!d\mu.$$ The same assertion holds for continuous functions satisfying the corresponding weak subharmonicity inequality.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-monotonicity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-monotonicity</summary>

*Proof.* Put $t=|x-p|$ and let $f\ge0$ be a smooth decreasing radial cutoff. Testing stationarity with $(x-p)f(t/r)$ and differentiating under the integral gives $$\frac{\mathop{}\!d}{\mathop{}\!d\log r}\left(r^{-m}\int f(t/r)\,\mathop{}\!d\mu\right)
 =r^{-m}\int \frac{-f'(t/r)}{rt}
                |(I-P_T)(x-p)|^2\,\mathop{}\!d\mu\ge0.$$ Approximation of the indicator of $[0,1)$ proves ball monotonicity. At a point where the rectifiable density is an integer, its limit at zero is at least $\omega_m$. Such points are dense in the support. Applying the conclusion at points tending to $p$, with radii increasing to $r$, proves <a href="#eq:lt-lower-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-lower-mass">[eq:lt-lower-mass]</a> at every support point.

Stationarity with $\varphi\nabla u$ shows $\int\nabla_Tu\cdot\nabla_T\varphi\,\mathop{}\!d\mu\le0$ for every nonnegative compactly supported $\varphi$. The preceding radial calculation, with its field multiplied by $u$, has the additional term $$r^{-m}\int f(t/r)\nabla_Tu\cdot(x-p)\,\mathop{}\!d\mu.$$ It is nonnegative: use the nonnegative radial primitive $\varphi(t)=\int_t^\infty a f(a/r)\,\mathop{}\!da$ in the weak inequality. Thus $r^{-m}\int_{\mathbf B_r(p)}u\,\mathop{}\!d\mu$ is nondecreasing. At integer-density points its limit is at least $\omega_m u(p)$. Continuity and the same approximation of the center prove <a href="#eq:lt-mean-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-mean-value">[eq:lt-mean-value]</a>. This argument uses only the weak inequality, and therefore proves the last assertion as well. ◻

</details>

We use a reference disk $S$ of class $C^J$, where the fixed finite order $J\ge6$ is chosen sufficiently large for all derivatives below, including the dimension-dependent test order in [lt:potential](#lt:potential). The disk has a fixed normal tube, small graph slope, and bounded coordinate derivatives through order $J$. All pulled-back first-variation fields have at least class $C^1$; Gaussian smoothing takes place in the base variables. The same fixed derivative order suffices under rescaling and throughout the induction. The flat case permits an arbitrary vertical translate. Restrict the varifold to a cylinder whose support is separated from its vertical boundary over every compact subset of the base. All fields used below have compact base support and are multiplied by a vertical cutoff equal to one on that support. Consequently this restriction has no vertical boundary term. Fixed enlargements of a working ball are always assumed to remain in this cylinder.

<a id="eq:lt-projection-identities"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-measures"></a>
Write a point of the tube as $q=y+z_{\rm phys}$, where $y=\pi(q)\in S$ and $z_{\rm phys}\in N_yS$. The second fundamental form is denoted by $\mathrm{II}$, its trace by $H_S$, and its shape operators by $$\langle A_v a,b\rangle=\langle\mathrm{II}(a,b),v\rangle,
 \qquad J_z=I-A_{z_{\rm phys}}.$$ All norms of $H_S$ and $\nabla^\perp H_S$ below are in the physical Euclidean metric. At $q$, decompose the tangent projection $P=P_{T_qV}$ relative to $T_yS\oplus N_yS$, and set <a id="eq:lt-tensors"></a>$$\label{eq:lt-tensors}
 B=I-P_{hh},\qquad e=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}B=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}P_{vv},\qquad
 \lambda=|z_{\rm phys}|^2+|H_S|^2+|\nabla^\perp H_S|^2.$$ Thus $B$ is a tensor at $q$, not a measure on the base. It is positive semidefinite, $0\le e\le m$, and the projection identities imply $$\label{eq:lt-projection-identities}
 P_{hv}P_{vh}=B-B^2,\qquad
 |P_{vh}a|^2\le B[a,a],\qquad |P_{vh}|\le C\sqrt e.$$ In physical coordinates the measures on the base are $$\label{eq:lt-measures}
 \nu=\pi_\#\mu,\quad M=\nu-Q\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S,\quad
 D=\pi_\#(e\mu),\quad \Lambda=\pi_\#(\lambda\mu),\quad
 L=2M-D,\quad \tau=|M|+D+\Lambda.$$ For a flat reference we set $\lambda=0$ and $\Lambda=0$. In particular a constant sheet offset is not charged as a geometric error in that case.

<a id="eq:lt-small-geometry"></a>
At the center of the original physical chart, normalize the base coordinates so that their metric is Euclidean there. For the coordinate immersion $F$, let $g=(DF)^\top DF$, choose the orthonormal horizontal frame $E=DF\,g^{-1/2}$ using the positive square root, and write $\mathsf C_y=g^{1/2}$; thus $DF\,X=E\mathsf C_yX$. Choose the normal frame by parallel transport along radial geodesics from the same center. This normal frame is the radial gauge for the original chart; its regularity and connection bounds follow from the fixed geometry above. When a physical base radius $l$ is scaled to one, we use base coordinates $y/l$ and height coordinates $\zeta=z_{\rm phys}/l$ in this fixed normal frame. The fixed coordinate bounds and the normalization at the center give $$|\mathsf C_y-I|+|D_{y/l}\mathsf C_y|+|D_{y/l}E|\le Cl$$ throughout the original chart. Measures, including the reference volume, are divided by $l^m$ and pushed forward to these coordinates. We retain the symbols in <a href="#eq:lt-measures" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-measures">[eq:lt-measures]</a> for the resulting measures. The function $\lambda$ is still evaluated in physical units; it is not replaced by $|\zeta|^2$ or by rescaled curvature. A further coordinate ball of radius $s$ consequently has physical radius $ls$. Define $$\label{eq:lt-small-geometry}
 \gamma=l+\sup|z_{\rm phys}|.$$ The reference disks used below have a uniformly small tube, so that $J_z$ and $J_z^{-1}$ are uniformly bounded.

<a id="stmt-lt-curvedvariation"></a>
<div id="lt:curvedvariation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lt-curvedvariation"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-scaledvariation"></a>
**Lemma 4** (Curved horizontal first variation). *For a compactly supported tangent field $X$ on $S$, $$\label{eq:lt-curvedvariation}
 \begin{split}
 &\left|\int\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_SX\,\mathop{}\!dM-\int B:\nabla^SX\,\mathop{}\!d\mu\right|\\
 &\quad\le C\int\Big\{|z_{\rm phys}|e|\nabla^SX|
 +\big[(|\nabla^\perp H_S|+|z_{\rm phys}|)|z_{\rm phys}|
       +\sqrt e\,|z_{\rm phys}|\big]|X|\Big\}\mathop{}\!d\mu.
 \end{split}$$ The integrands containing tensors are evaluated at $q$, with the base field pulled back by $\pi$. In coordinates scaled by $l$, the right side is bounded by $$\label{eq:lt-scaledvariation}
 C\gamma\int(e+\lambda)(|\nabla X|+|X|)\,\mathop{}\!d\mu.$$ Here and below a dimensionless scaled test field is used in a scaled identity.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-curvedvariation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-curvedvariation</summary>

*Proof.* The nearest-point derivative is $D\pi=J_z^{-1}P_h$. Lift $X$ to the ambient field $\overline X(q)=J_zX(y)$. This is the velocity obtained by moving $y$ with velocity $X$ and transporting $z_{\rm phys}$ parallel in the normal connection. Compute at a point in tangent and normal frames whose respective connections vanish there. With $K_z(a)=(\nabla_a A)_{z_{\rm phys}}X$, the four blocks of its derivative are $$\begin{aligned}
 (D\overline X)_{hh}&=J_z(\nabla X)J_z^{-1}-K_zJ_z^{-1},&
 (D\overline X)_{hv}v&=-A_vX,\\
 (D\overline X)_{vh}a&=\mathrm{II}(J_z^{-1}a,J_zX),&
 (D\overline X)_{vv}&=0.
\end{aligned}$$ The first term of the horizontal block has trace $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_SX$. Codazzi in Euclidean space gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}K_z=\langle\nabla_X^\perp H_S,z_{\rm phys}\rangle.$$ For completeness, Codazzi follows by writing $\mathrm{II}_{ij}=(\partial_i\partial_jF)^\perp$ in local coordinates and commuting the three derivatives of the immersion; at the chosen point the connection terms are precisely those in the covariant derivative. Since $K_z=O(|z_{\rm phys}||X|)$ and $J_z^{-1}-I=O(|z_{\rm phys}|)$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(D\overline X)_{hh}
 =\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_SX-\langle\nabla_X^\perp H_S,z_{\rm phys}\rangle
       +O(|z_{\rm phys}|^2|X|).$$ The symmetric sum of the off-diagonal blocks, paired with $a\in T_yS$ and $v\in N_yS$, is exactly $$\langle a,J_z^{-1}[A_{z_{\rm phys}},A_v]X\rangle.$$ Indeed it equals $\langle a,(J_z^{-1}A_vJ_z-A_v)X\rangle$. Its size is $O(|z_{\rm phys}||a||v||X|)$, including in arbitrary codimension. Contracting it with $P_{vh}$ costs $C\sqrt e\,|z_{\rm phys}||X|$ by <a href="#eq:lt-projection-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-projection-identities">[eq:lt-projection-identities]</a>. Contracting the horizontal block with $I-B$ instead of the identity gives $-B:\nabla X$ and an error at most $C|z_{\rm phys}|e(|\nabla X|+|X|)$. Absorb its last summand in the preceding $\sqrt e$ term. Integrating $P:D\overline X=0$ and using $\int_S\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_SX\,\mathop{}\!d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S=0$ proves <a href="#eq:lt-curvedvariation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-curvedvariation">[eq:lt-curvedvariation]</a>.

After scaling, the coefficients of $|\nabla X|$ and $|X|$ are respectively $$|z_{\rm phys}|e,\qquad
 l\big[(|\nabla^\perp H_S|+|z_{\rm phys}|)|z_{\rm phys}|
                         +\sqrt e\,|z_{\rm phys}|\big].$$ Young’s inequality bounds them by $C\gamma(e+\lambda)$. Bounded coordinate changes preserve this estimate. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lt-jacobian"></a>
<div id="lt:jacobian" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lt-jacobian"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-integer-measure"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-signed-jacobian"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Projection and integer counts). *The tangential Jacobian of $\pi$ is $$\label{eq:lt-jacobian}
 J_\pi=\frac{\sqrt{\det P_{hh}}}{\det J_z},\qquad
 |J_\pi-1|\le C(e+\lambda).$$ There is a nonnegative integer-valued function $N$ on the base, finite almost everywhere, such that $$\label{eq:lt-integer-measure}
 \pi_\#(J_\pi\mu)=N\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S,
 \qquad |\nu-N\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S|\le C(D+\Lambda).$$ The inequalities are measure inequalities on every relatively compact base set. Moreover, for sufficiently small $e$, $$\label{eq:lt-signed-jacobian}
 2(1-J_\pi)-e\le Ce^2+C(|z_{\rm phys}|^2+|H_S|^2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-jacobian"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-jacobian</summary>

*Proof.* The squared Jacobian of the orthogonal projection of $T$ onto $T_yS$ is $\det P_{hh}$. Composition with $J_z^{-1}$ proves the formula. If the eigenvalues of $B$ are $b_i\in[0,1]$, then $1-\sqrt{\prod_i(1-b_i)}\le\sum_i b_i=e$. Also $$\det J_z=1-\langle H_S,z_{\rm phys}\rangle+O(|z_{\rm phys}|^2).$$ These facts and Young’s inequality prove the bound in <a href="#eq:lt-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-jacobian">[eq:lt-jacobian]</a>. Expanding the square root one order further gives $\sqrt{\det(I-B)}=1-e/2+O(e^2)$, which proves <a href="#eq:lt-signed-jacobian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-signed-jacobian">[eq:lt-signed-jacobian]</a>. The rectifiable area formula (Simon 2018, chap. 3, equation (2.5)) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[Simon2018](#ref-Simon2018)] gives the first equality in <a href="#eq:lt-integer-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-integer-measure">[eq:lt-integer-measure]</a>, where $N$ counts points of nonzero projection Jacobian with their multiplicities. Its second assertion follows by pushing forward $(1-J_\pi)\mu$. The part on which the projection has deficient rank has $e\ge1$ and is included in this error; no absolute-continuity assertion about its pushforward is needed. ◻

</details>

The next elementary analytic observation explains why a scalar weak-$L^1$ estimate is sufficient here. Both its distributional estimate and the integer count are essential. Its proof uses the Calderón–Zygmund decomposition and cancellation argument (Calderón and Zygmund 1952, chap. I, Lemmas 1–2) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[CalderonZygmund1952](#ref-CalderonZygmund1952)], with a localized potential and a harmonic remainder controlled modulo constants.

<a id="stmt-lt-potential"></a>
<div id="lt:potential" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lt-gradient-equation"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-weak-one"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-distribution-control"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Scalar potential estimate). *Suppose a finite scalar measure $v$ on $B_4\subset\mathbb R^m$ satisfies $$\label{eq:lt-gradient-equation}
 \partial_jv=\sum_i\partial_i F_{ij}+g_j,
 \qquad \sum_{ij}|F_{ij}|(B_4)+\sum_j|g_j|(B_4)\le\epsilon.$$ Then there is a real constant $c$ such that the density $v_{\rm ac}$ obeys $$\label{eq:lt-weak-one}
 |\{y\in B_2:|v_{\rm ac}(y)-c|>t\}|
                     \le C\epsilon/t\quad(t>0),$$ and, for every smooth test supported in $B_2$, $$\label{eq:lt-distribution-control}
 |\langle v-c\,\mathop{}\!dy,\varphi\rangle|
                     \le C\epsilon\|\varphi\|_{C^{k}},$$ where $k$ is a fixed integer depending only on $m$. Fixed changes of the radii give the same conclusion.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-potential"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-potential</summary>

*Proof.* First mollify on a slightly smaller domain. Choose a cutoff equal to one on $B_3$ and supported in $B_{7/2}$, and let $\Phi$ be the fundamental solution of the Laplacian. The potential $$u=\sum_{ij}\partial_i\partial_j\Phi*(\chi F_{ij})
                       +\sum_j\partial_j\Phi*(\chi g_j)$$ has the same Laplacian as $v$ on $B_3$, so $h=v-u$ is harmonic there. The second-derivative kernels have the weak-type estimate $|\{|u|>t\}\cap B_3|\le C\epsilon/t$; the first-derivative kernels are integrable on bounded sets. We recall the kernel argument. Decompose a mollified finite measure at level $t$ into its bounded part and zero-mean pieces on maximal dyadic cubes. The bounded part has squared $L^2$ norm at most $Ct$ times the original mass norm. The Fourier multiplier $\xi_i\xi_j/|\xi|^2$ has $L^2$ norm at most one. The bad cubes have total volume at most $C\epsilon/t$; off their fixed enlargements, cancellation and the bound $|\nabla\partial_i\partial_j\Phi(x)|\le C|x|^{-m-1}$ bound the $L^1$ norm of their contributions by $C\epsilon$. Chebyshev’s inequality proves the stated weak estimate, uniformly in mollification. Pairing the same potentials against a smooth test, moving the derivatives to the test, also gives $|\langle u,\varphi\rangle|\le
C\epsilon\|\varphi\|_{C^k}$ on any fixed bounded region.

The harmonic remainder is controlled modulo a constant by the full gradient equation. For $x\in B_{5/2}$ choose a fixed radial unit-integral bump $\eta_x$ supported in a small ball about $x$. Harmonic mean value and <a href="#eq:lt-gradient-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-gradient-equation">[eq:lt-gradient-equation]</a> give $$\partial_jh(x)=\langle\partial_jh,\eta_x\rangle
   =-\sum_i\langle F_{ij},\partial_i\eta_x\rangle
      +\langle g_j,\eta_x\rangle
      +\langle u,\partial_j\eta_x\rangle.$$ Consequently $\sup_{B_{5/2}}|\nabla h|\le C\epsilon$. Taking $c=h(0)$ proves both estimates for the mollified quantities. The constants $c$ are bounded by pairing against one fixed unit-integral bump and the finite mass of $v$. Pass to a subsequence as the mollification tends to zero; the densities converge almost everywhere at Lebesgue points. This proves <a href="#eq:lt-weak-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-weak-one">[eq:lt-weak-one]</a>, and weak convergence against tests proves <a href="#eq:lt-distribution-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-distribution-control">[eq:lt-distribution-control]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lt-scalar"></a>
<div id="lt:scalar" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lt-scalar-mass"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Scalar mass and propagation of the integer). *Fix a bound for $Q$ and fixed concentric interior enlargements of a ball $B_s$. Suppose the normalized projected mass on the outer enlargement is bounded, and $$\epsilon=s^{-m}(D+\Lambda)(B_{cs})$$ is sufficiently small; $c>1$ is a fixed enlargement with room for the argument. The count selects a unique integer $q\ge0$ on most of an intermediate ball. If either a fixed smooth mass average differs from $Q$ by less than $1/4$, or $N=Q$ on a fixed majority of that ball, then $q=Q$. In that case $$\label{eq:lt-scalar-mass}
 |M|(B_s)+\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S\{y\in B_s:N(y)\ne Q\}
                       \le C(D+\Lambda)(B_{cs}).$$ In the smooth-average condition the test is nonnegative, has reference integral one, and has fixed scaled derivative bounds. The same integer propagates along overlapping comparable balls for which these small-energy and mass bounds hold and whose overlap has a fixed positive relative volume.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-scalar"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-scalar</summary>

*Proof.* Scale to $s=1$. Write $\mathop{}\!d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S=a(y)\,\mathop{}\!dy$ and put the factor $a^{-1}$ in each coordinate component of the tangent test field. Then [lt:curvedvariation](#lt:curvedvariation) gives an equation of the form <a href="#eq:lt-gradient-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-gradient-equation">[eq:lt-gradient-equation]</a> for the scalar measure $a^{-1}\nu$. The divergence data have mass at most $C(D+\Lambda)$, as do the zeroth-order data; derivatives of the fixed coordinate factors have bounded size. In particular, no derivative of the unknown scalar mass is estimated separately. Apply [lt:potential](#lt:potential). By [lt:jacobian](#lt:jacobian), the difference between $a^{-1}\nu$ and $N\,\mathop{}\!dy$ has mass at most $C\epsilon$. The density version of this difference and <a href="#eq:lt-weak-one" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-weak-one">[eq:lt-weak-one]</a> give $$|\{|N-c|>t\}\cap B_2|\le C\epsilon/t.$$ If $\epsilon=0$, this estimate for every $t>0$ gives $N=c$ almost everywhere. Since $N$ is integer-valued, $c$ is an integer, and the distributional bound is exact; the conclusions follow directly. Assume henceforth that $\epsilon>0$. Taking $t=C_0\epsilon$ with $C_0$ large proves that, on a fixed large majority, $N$ is within $C_0\epsilon$ of $c$. For small $\epsilon$ there is at most one integer in this interval; call it $q$. Thus $|c-q|\le C\epsilon$. The distributional bound against the specified smooth average makes its value $q+O(\epsilon)$, so the smooth-average hypothesis forces $q=Q$. The majority hypothesis has the same consequence by intersecting the two majority sets.

<a id="eq:lt-count-exception"></a>
Take now $t$ to be a fixed fraction of one. Since $|c-Q|\le C\epsilon$, $$\label{eq:lt-count-exception}
 |\{N\ne Q\}\cap B_2|\le C\epsilon.$$ The nonnegativity of $N$ gives $\int_{B_2}(N-Q)_-\,\mathop{}\!dy\le Q|\{N\ne Q\}\cap B_2|$. The small measure error between $\nu$ and $N\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S$ therefore bounds $M_-(B_2)$ by $C\epsilon$. Choose $0\le\varphi\le1$, equal to one on $B_1$ and supported in $B_2$. Equation <a href="#eq:lt-distribution-control" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-distribution-control">[eq:lt-distribution-control]</a>, applied also to the bounded volume factor, gives $|\int\varphi\,\mathop{}\!dM|\le C\epsilon$. Hence $$M_+(B_1)\le\int\varphi\,\mathop{}\!dM+\int\varphi\,\mathop{}\!dM_-
                                                    \le C\epsilon.$$ This proves total variation and the exceptional-volume assertion, and rescaling proves <a href="#eq:lt-scalar-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-scalar-mass">[eq:lt-scalar-mass]</a>. Finally, on an overlap of fixed relative volume, the same fixed-threshold argument gives $|\{N\ne q_i\}|\le C\epsilon_i$ for each ball’s selected integer $q_i$, before any identification with $Q$. These exceptional sets cannot fill the overlap. At a remaining point both selected integers equal $N$. Induction along a finite chain proves propagation. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-rem-lt-scalar-radii"></a>
<div id="rem:lt-scalar-radii" class="remark">

**Remark 8**. The enlargement constant in <a href="#eq:lt-scalar-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-scalar-mass">[eq:lt-scalar-mass]</a> can be any prescribed $c>1$, at the cost of constants depending on the interior room. Applying the argument on a finite cover gives, in particular, the notation $|M|(B)\le C(D+\Lambda)(2B)$. It is never an estimate on a ball with no interior enlargement or with an unspecified integer.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Configurations and height bounds

<a id="stmt-lt-config"></a>
<div id="lt:config" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Configurations, higher moments, and support heights). *Fix $Q\ge1$, and choose a sufficiently large fixed enlargement $c>1$ depending only on the dimensions, $Q$, and the fixed coordinate and cylinder-room data. For every prescribed exceptional proportion $\delta_0>0$ there is a threshold $d_0=d_0(\delta_0)>0$, depending otherwise only on these fixed data, such that the following holds. Suppose $Q$ is selected on the enlarged ball $B_{cs}(x)$ and $$s^{-m}\tau(B_{cs}(x))\le d\le d_0,$$ with the cylinder and interior-room conventions above. There is a configuration $\mathcal A=\{a_1,\ldots,a_Q\}$ of scaled heights such that, outside a subset of $B_s(x)$ of relative volume at most $\delta_0$, the fiber consists of exactly $Q$ projection entries and can be matched to $\mathcal A$ within $C_{\delta_0}s\sqrt d$. Configurations on comparable overlapping balls can be chosen, and any such majority configurations can be matched, within the sum of these bounds, provided the exceptional proportions are smaller than a fixed fraction of the overlap.*

<a id="eq:lt-p-range"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-config-moment"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-config-sup"></a>
*For every fixed finite $p>2$ satisfying $$\label{eq:lt-p-range}
 m(1/2-1/p)<1,$$ and every fixed $0<\beta<\min\{1/2,1/m\}$, this same configuration satisfies $$\begin{aligned}
 \left(s^{-m}\int_{\pi^{-1}(B_s(x))}
       \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\zeta,\mathcal A)^p\,\mathop{}\!d\mu\right)^{1/p}
       &\le C_p s\sqrt d,\label{eq:lt-config-moment}\\
 \sup_{\mathop{\mathrm{spt}}\mu\cap\pi^{-1}(B_s(x))}
       \mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\zeta,\mathcal A)&\le C_\beta s d^\beta.
       \label{eq:lt-config-sup}
\end{aligned}$$ The enlargement $c$, threshold $d_0$, and configuration do not depend on $p$ or $\beta$. The constants $C_p,C_\beta$ may depend on their indicated exponents and the fixed data, but not on $\delta_0$; the majority constant $C_{\delta_0}$ may depend on it. The support estimate holds for all support points, including points with zero projection Jacobian.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-config"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-config</summary>

<a id="eq:lt-multiplier-oscillation"></a>
*Proof.* It is enough to prove the estimates for $d>0$. If the actual energy is zero, fix $0<\delta'<\min\{\delta_0,1/2\}$ and positive upper bounds $d_j\downarrow0$ below $d_0(\delta')$. Let $G_j$ be the measurable good sets from the positive case. Fatou’s lemma applied to their complements gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S(\limsup_jG_j)\ge(1-\delta')\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S(B_s(x)).$$ Choose a defined fiber $\mathcal F_0$ in this limsup and retain the indices $j_k$ for which it is good. Its $Q$ entries match the corresponding configurations within $C_{\delta'}s\sqrt{d_{j_k}}\to0$, so those configurations converge to $\mathcal F_0$ up to permutations. The limsup of the selected $G_{j_k}$ has the same volume bound. On each fiber in that limsup, the matching triangle inequality along its good subsubsequence gives exact matching to $\mathcal F_0$. For one admissible $\beta_0>0$, the support estimate and the matching convergence put every support height in $\mathcal F_0$. All support distances and hence all finite moments are zero, proving the assertion for $d=0$. We give the finite selection argument before the iteration that controls exceptional fibers. If $f$ is a bounded Lipschitz function of height with $|f|\le1$, use $f(\zeta)\overline X$ in the horizontal first variation. In flat coordinates the additional term is $\nabla f\cdot P_{vh}X$. In normal coordinates its additional frame term is bounded by $C\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(f)l|z_{\rm phys}||X|$. In the curved case, as in [lt:scalar](#lt:scalar), write $\mathop{}\!d\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S=a\,\mathop{}\!dy$ and take the coordinate components $X=a^{-1}Y$. Then $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_SX=a^{-1}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}Y$ exactly; derivatives of $a^{-1}$ remain in the tensor and geometric terms. Cauchy–Schwarz, the normalized mass bound, and [lt:potential](#lt:potential), [lt:jacobian](#lt:jacobian) show that the fiber function $$N_f(y)=\sum_{q\in\pi^{-1}(y),\ J_\pi(q)>0}\theta(q)f(\zeta(q))$$ differs from its fixed smooth average $\overline N_f$ by a weak-$L^1$ quantity whose normalized size is at most $$\label{eq:lt-multiplier-oscillation}
 C\{d+s\mathop{\mathrm{Lip}}(f)\sqrt d\}.$$ The same bound controls distributional pairings. Changing between Jacobian-weighted and ordinary mass in this statement costs $Cd$.

For this finite selection prescribe $0<\delta\le1/4$; a larger requested exceptional proportion is covered by using $\delta=1/4$. Normalize the smoothly averaged vertical counting measure to a probability measure $\mathcal P$; its total before normalization is $Q+O(d)$ by [lt:scalar](#lt:scalar). Fix a mass mesh $\eta_0$ much smaller than $1/Q$. For each of the $n$ coordinates choose its quantiles at levels $j\eta_0$, and test smoothed half-lines immediately to both sides of these quantiles, with transition width $w=K s\sqrt d$. Repeated quantiles cause no problem: retain both one-sided tests. There are at most $C(n)/\eta_0$ tests. For each, the right side of <a href="#eq:lt-multiplier-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-multiplier-oscillation">[eq:lt-multiplier-oscillation]</a> is $C(d+K^{-1})$. First choose $\eta_0$, then choose $K$ large enough for these tests and the joint-count tests below, and finally choose a positive energy threshold small. The union of the exceptional sets where any test differs from its average by more than $\eta_0$ then has relative volume at most $\delta/3$.

Also discard the set where $N\ne Q$. By <a href="#eq:lt-count-exception" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-count-exception">[eq:lt-count-exception]</a> its relative volume is $O(d)$, so it is at most $\delta/3$ after decreasing the same threshold. Every remaining fiber therefore has exactly $Q$ projection entries, counted with multiplicity.

Let $F_y$ be the empirical coordinate distribution on a remaining fiber (normalized by $Q$), and let $F$ be that of $\mathcal P$. Bracketing an arbitrary real number by adjacent quantiles gives $$F(t-2w)-3\eta_0\le F_y(t)\le F(t+2w)+3\eta_0.$$ If $t$ is in a quantile jump, the left and right tests at that quantile give the same inequalities; thus atoms require no continuity assumption. Comparison of two good fibers gives this estimate with $4w$ and $6\eta_0$. For $6\eta_0<1/(2Q)$, comparison at the levels $(j-1/2)/Q$ shows that their ordered coordinate lists differ by at most $Cw$. Choose one such fiber as a temporary reference. In each coordinate group its at most $Q$ levels by joining consecutive levels whose gap is at most a sufficiently large fixed multiple of $w$. Each resulting interval has length at most $CQw$, and distinct intervals have an empty gap in which a cutoff can change from zero to one.

Coordinate lists alone do not determine vector configurations. To determine the joint counts, test products of the interval cutoffs just constructed. There are at most $Q^n$ such tests and their Lipschitz constants are at most $C/w$. On the good coordinate fibers every product equals an exact indicator. After decreasing the threshold if necessary and discarding a further relative volume at most $\delta/3$, each joint count is within less than $1/4$ of its smooth average. It is an integer, so it is the same on all remaining fibers. Taking this many copies of one point in each occupied coordinate box gives $\mathcal A$. The boxes have diameter $Cw$, proving the majority assertion. Intersecting the good sets of two overlapping balls proves the matching assertion. Write $d_{\rm sel}(\delta)>0$ for a threshold sufficient for this finite selection, and use $d_{\rm sel}(1/4)$ when $\delta>1/4$.

There is also an exact finite selection when the energy on this ball is zero. Fix $0<\delta'<\min\{\delta,1/2\}$, apply only the finite selection just proved with positive upper bounds $d_j\downarrow0$ below $d_{\rm sel}(\delta')$, and use the limsup construction and matching through one common fiber from the start of this proof. That part uses only the good sets and their matching errors, not the moment or support estimates. It yields an exact majority configuration with exceptional proportion at most $\delta'<\delta$.

The dyadic covering geometry below, including its enlargement and overlap ratios, is fixed from the data at the outset and determines $c$ in the statement. Choose an internal proportion $0<\delta_{\rm int}<1/4$ so that two exceptional sets cannot fill any comparison overlap, and small enough for the support argument. Then choose a fixed threshold $d_{\rm int}\le d_{\rm sel}(\delta_{\rm int})$ small enough for that argument and the iteration, and set $$d_0(\delta_0)=\min\{d_{\rm int},d_{\rm sel}(\delta_0)\}.$$ For every selection ball in the descent, including the root, use the actual normalized mass of $\tau$ on the associated fixed enlargement as the selection upper bound, using the exact finite selection when that mass is zero. Use the root configuration $\mathcal A$ selected with $\delta_{\rm int}$ throughout the descent. If $\delta_0<\delta_{\rm int}$, apply the finite selection once more at the root with exceptional proportion $\delta_0$ and the same actual energy. A common good fiber matches the two root configurations within $C_{\delta_0}s\sqrt d$, after enlarging that constant. On the good fibers of the second selection, the triangle inequality then gives the prescribed majority bound for $\mathcal A$. If $\delta_0\ge\delta_{\rm int}$, the original good set suffices. Thus the descent and its root configuration use only the fixed internal choices; the finer selection affects only the majority constant and input threshold.

We next record how to include every support point. On a continuing ball of radius $r$ with normalized $\tau$ at most $d_{\rm int}$, the majority assertion and [lt:scalar](#lt:scalar), [lt:jacobian](#lt:jacobian) bound the mass outside the configuration’s height neighborhoods by $$C(\delta_{\rm int}+d_{\rm int})r^m.$$ Indeed the mass above bad fibers is at most their $Q$-fold base volume plus $|M|$, and the projection error also has mass at most $C(D+\Lambda)$. Because the restricted support is separated from the artificial vertical boundary, its extension by zero across that boundary remains stationary for fields supported away from the lateral boundary; multiply such a field by a vertical cutoff equal to one near the restricted support to verify this assertion. The monotonicity ball is taken in this extension and remains in the lateral working region; it need not lie inside the original vertical cutoff. If a support point over an inner base ball were at distance at least $c_1r$ from these height neighborhoods, its ambient ball of radius $c_2r$ would lie outside them. For fixed small $c_2>0$, this contradicts <a href="#eq:lt-lower-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-lower-mass">[eq:lt-lower-mass]</a> when $\delta_{\rm int},d_{\rm int}$ are sufficiently small. The same reasoning works in the curved tube since its coordinate map and inverse are uniformly Lipschitz. This proves an $O(r)$ bound for every support point on an interior ball. In particular it applies to a first stopped child, using its continuing parent.

For the quantitative iteration, subdivide the inner base region into dyadic cubes, using around each cube a ball of a fixed large multiple of its side. Start with sufficiently generous interior room. Continue down a branch while the normalized $\tau$ on these balls is at most the fixed threshold $d_{\rm int}$. Otherwise stop at the first such cube. On continuing balls the normalized mass is bounded by $Q+Cd_{\rm int}$, the integer is $Q$, and the majority configurations at a child and its parent match with increment $$Cr\sqrt{r^{-m}\tau(\text{fixed enlargement of the parent})}.$$ The fixed parent enlargement here contains both selection enlargements. If its $\tau$-mass is zero, both actual selection energies vanish, their exact majority configurations match on the overlap, and the child cannot stop. At a first stopped cube the all-support argument gives instead $Cr$ for the distance from each support height to the parent’s configuration. Its stopping condition charges $r^m$ to a fixed multiple of $\tau$ on that enlargement. Configurations are regarded with their optimal matching, so their nearest-point distances obey the triangle inequality even at coincident entries.

<a id="eq:lt-increment-two"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-increment-sup"></a>
Write $r_j=2^{-j}s$, and let $b_j(q)$ be the corresponding increment for a point above a continuing cube, or its final error at its first stopped cube; put $b_j=0$ after a stop. Fixed enlargements of cubes have bounded overlap at each generation. The preceding charge, the bounded mass of a continuing parent, and $\tau(B_{cs})\le d s^m$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 s^{-m}\int b_j^2\,\mathop{}\!d\mu&\le C r_j^2d,
                         \label{eq:lt-increment-two}\\
 \|b_j\|_\infty&\le C r_j
          \min\{1,2^{jm/2}\sqrt d\}.
                         \label{eq:lt-increment-sup}
\end{aligned}$$ For the second bound on stopped cubes, stopping itself implies $2^{jm}d\ge c>0$, so its $Cr_j$ error has the required form. For a branch which never stops, the all-support $Cr_j$ bound tends to zero; thus its configurations approach the actual support height in distance. It follows in both cases that $\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(\zeta(q),\mathcal A)\le\sum_jb_j(q)$.

The covering, stopping decisions, and configuration were fixed before either exponent was chosen. The following summations therefore apply to this same $\mathcal A$ for every exponent in the stated ranges. Interpolate <a href="#eq:lt-increment-two" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-increment-two">[eq:lt-increment-two]</a> with the second part of <a href="#eq:lt-increment-sup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-increment-sup">[eq:lt-increment-sup]</a>, without the minimum, to obtain $$\left(s^{-m}\int b_j^p\,\mathop{}\!d\mu\right)^{1/p}
 \le Cs\sqrt d\,2^{-j[1-m(1/2-1/p)]}.$$ Minkowski’s inequality and <a href="#eq:lt-p-range" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-p-range">[eq:lt-p-range]</a> prove <a href="#eq:lt-config-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-config-moment">[eq:lt-config-moment]</a>. Summing <a href="#eq:lt-increment-sup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-increment-sup">[eq:lt-increment-sup]</a> above and below $2^{-j}\simeq d^{1/m}$ gives $Cs\sqrt d$ when $m=1$, $Cs\sqrt d(1+|\log d|)$ when $m=2$, and $Cs d^{1/m}$ when $m>2$. Each is bounded by $C_\beta s d^\beta$ for the stated range of $\beta$, proving <a href="#eq:lt-config-sup" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-config-sup">[eq:lt-config-sup]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Gaussian and height identities

<a id="stmt-lt-weightedballs"></a>
<div id="lt:weightedballs" class="corollary">

<a id="eq:lt-weighted-scalar"></a>
**Corollary 10** (Gaussian weights and polynomial dilations). *Define $$G_s(y)=(2\pi s^2)^{-m/2}\exp(-|y|^2/(2s^2))$$ and fix a center $x$. On the region $|y-x|\le Rs$, cover the base by balls of radii comparable to $s/(1+|y-x|/s)$, with fixed enlarged balls having bounded overlap. Suppose each enlarged ball meets the hypotheses of [lt:scalar](#lt:scalar) with the same propagated integer $Q$. Then $$\label{eq:lt-weighted-scalar}
 \int_{|y-x|\le Rs}G_s(y-x)\,\mathop{}\!d|M|(y)
 \le C\int_{|y-x|\le (R+C)s}G_s(y-x)\,\mathop{}\!d(D+\Lambda)(y).$$ The constant is independent of $R$. The part outside the indicated region is retained as a tail error.*

<a id="eq:lt-polynomial-dilation"></a>
*For the configuration conclusion, fix one exceptional proportion $\delta_{\rm cmp}>0$ smaller than the fixed comparison-overlap proportions. If, in addition, on all admissible dilations $1\le t\le R_*$ one has $$\label{eq:lt-polynomial-dilation}
 (ts)^{-m}\tau(B_{cts}(x))\le C t^Jd,$$ with $Ct^Jd\le d_0(\delta_{\rm cmp})$ throughout this range and the same integer propagated, then configurations matched from the scale $s$ have moment and support estimates on a dilation $t$ equal to those in [lt:config](#lt:config), with an additional fixed power of $t$. Gaussian integrals of these estimates have constants depending on $J$ and the fixed moment order, once the tail beyond $R_*$ is accounted for.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-weightedballs"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-weightedballs</summary>

*Proof.* The logarithmic derivative of $G_s$ is $-(y-x)/s^2$. Its values on each fixed enlargement of a ball of the stated radius differ by bounded factors. Such a cover can be obtained by a maximal disjoint family of sufficiently small balls for this radius function; nearby radii are comparable. Apply <a href="#eq:lt-scalar-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-scalar-mass">[eq:lt-scalar-mass]</a> on each ball and sum. This proves <a href="#eq:lt-weighted-scalar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-weighted-scalar">[eq:lt-weighted-scalar]</a> without a factor depending on the number of balls. Apply [lt:config](#lt:config) with the fixed proportion $\delta_{\rm cmp}$ successively to concentric dyadic dilations and to overlapping comparable balls. Every increment is bounded by a fixed power of $t$ times $s\sqrt d$, and each local moment estimate has the same property by <a href="#eq:lt-polynomial-dilation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-polynomial-dilation">[eq:lt-polynomial-dilation]</a>. Summation of the geometric increments gives the claimed polynomial bounds. Finally $\sum_{j\ge0}2^{jC}\exp(-c4^j)<\infty$ absorbs any fixed polynomial loss in the Gaussian integral. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:lt-chart-tail"></a>
We specify the cutoff convention for every Gaussian identity. Choose a smooth base cutoff $\chi$ which is one on the region under consideration and has compact support in the chart. A subscript $s$ denotes convolution at $x$ with $G_s$ of the extended measure; on the chart it is the original measure, and outside it any stated extension is used. In a first-variation identity the test field is multiplied by $\chi$. Contributions where $\chi\ne1$, including the difference from the chosen extension, are denoted by $\mathcal T_s$. If the cutoff annulus has distance at least $h$ from $x$ and the relevant total measure is at most $K_0$, decrease $h$ to the available transition width if necessary, and choose $0\le\chi\le1$ with $\|\nabla\chi\|_\infty\le C(1+h^{-1})$. The first-variation product rule differentiates this fixed cutoff once; scale derivatives leave it fixed. For $0<s\le1$, direct differentiation of the Gaussian bounds each such term by $$\label{eq:lt-chart-tail}
 C K_0 s^{-C}(1+h^{-1})^C\exp(-h^2/(4s^2)),$$ times the stated bounds for the height multiplier and its derivatives. One may use a larger fixed exponent $C$ for the finite set of Gaussian and height-multiplier derivatives used below. When a polynomial dilation bound such as <a href="#eq:lt-polynomial-dilation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-polynomial-dilation">[eq:lt-polynomial-dilation]</a> is available through $R_*$, annular summation bounds the contribution from $Rs<|y-x|\le R_*s$ by $C\,\mathrm{poly}(R)e^{-cR^2}$ times its reference density. The part beyond $R_*s$ remains a separate tail, including any part still inside the chart, until a further dilation or total-mass bound controls it. The exterior-chart term is also retained. A periodized Gaussian on a fixed large torus obeys the same estimates. These formulas are the meaning of a negligible Gaussian tail; no unrestricted extension is silently discarded.

<a id="stmt-lt-gaussian"></a>
<div id="lt:gaussian" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lt-gaussian-flat"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-gaussian-curved"></a>
**Lemma 11** (Gaussian mass identity). *Set $u=(y-x)/s$, $A(s)=s^2M_s(x)$, and $$R_s(x)=\int G_s(y-x)B[u,u]\,\mathop{}\!d\mu.$$ In flat coordinates, apart from $\mathcal T_s$, $$\label{eq:lt-gaussian-flat}
 \dot A=s^2L_s+s^2R_s,
 \qquad \dot{}=\frac{\mathop{}\!d}{\mathop{}\!d\log s}.$$ In the curved coordinates fixed above, $$\label{eq:lt-gaussian-curved}
 \dot A=s^2L_s+s^2R_s+\mathcal E_s+\mathcal T_s,
 \qquad
 |\mathcal E_s|\le C\gamma s^2
           \int G_s(y-x)(1+|u|^2)\,\mathop{}\!d\tau(y).$$ Here the entries of $u$ in $B[u,u]$ are taken in the chosen orthonormal horizontal frame, and $\gamma$ retains its definition from the fixed physical coordinates.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-gaussian"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-gaussian</summary>

*Proof.* In flat coordinates $\dot G_s=-\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_y((y-x)G_s)$. The horizontal first variation says $\int\mathop{\mathrm{div}}X\,\mathop{}\!dM=\int B:DX\,\mathop{}\!d\mu$. Take $X=(y-x)G_s$. Since $DX=G_s(I-u\otimes u)$, it follows that $\dot M_s=-D_s+R_s$. Differentiating the factor $s^2$ proves <a href="#eq:lt-gaussian-flat" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-gaussian-flat">[eq:lt-gaussian-flat]</a>.

For the curved identity apply [lt:curvedvariation](#lt:curvedvariation) to the same coordinate field. Its scaled derivative is bounded by $CG_s(1+|u|^2)$ and its size by $CsG_s|u|$. On a physical chart of radius $l$, coordinate coefficients and their scaled first derivatives differ from the centered Euclidean ones by $O(l)$. The divergence comparison is made after subtracting $Q\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}_S$; its coefficient error therefore multiplies $M$, not the background mass. The tensor coefficient errors multiply $B$ and are bounded by $C\gamma e$. Equation <a href="#eq:lt-scaledvariation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-scaledvariation">[eq:lt-scaledvariation]</a> controls the remaining geometric terms. Multiplying by $s^2$ proves <a href="#eq:lt-gaussian-curved" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-gaussian-curved">[eq:lt-gaussian-curved]</a>. Differentiation of $\chi$ produces precisely the tails described in <a href="#eq:lt-chart-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-chart-tail">[eq:lt-chart-tail]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lt-heighttests"></a>
<div id="lt:heighttests" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:lt-height-horizontal"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-height-normal"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-height-gaussian"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Height tests and geometric errors). *In flat coordinates, for a base test $\varphi$, a scalar height function $f$, a horizontal field $X$, and a normal vector function $v$, stationarity gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int f\mathop{\mathrm{div}}X\,\mathop{}\!d\mu
 &=\int f B:DX\,\mathop{}\!d\mu-
                   \int Df\cdot P_{vh}X\,\mathop{}\!d\mu,
                     \label{eq:lt-height-horizontal}\\
 0&=\int\{\varphi Dv:P_{vv}
                +v\cdot P_{vh}\nabla\varphi\}\,\mathop{}\!d\mu.
                     \label{eq:lt-height-normal}
\end{aligned}$$ All height functions here use the scaled variable $\zeta$. For a scale-independent $C^2$ height function $\Gamma$, these formulas give $$\label{eq:lt-height-gaussian}
 \frac{\mathop{}\!d}{\mathop{}\!d\log s}\int G_s\Gamma\,\mathop{}\!d\mu
 =\int G_s\Gamma(B[u,u]-e)\,\mathop{}\!d\mu
                       +s^2\int G_sD^2\Gamma:P_{vv}\,\mathop{}\!d\mu,$$ apart from the specified chart tails. If $\Gamma$ depends on $s$, add $\int G_s\dot\Gamma\,\mathop{}\!d\mu$.*

<a id="eq:lt-normal-error"></a>
<a id="eq:lt-horizontal-height-error"></a>
*In the curved tube, use the original radial-gauge normal frame fixed above. The extra trace terms in a normal test $\varphi v(\zeta)$ without a derivative of $\varphi$ are bounded pointwise by $C|\varphi|$ times $$\label{eq:lt-normal-error}
 |Dv|l|z_{\rm phys}|\sqrt e
       +l|v|\bigl(|H_S|+|z_{\rm phys}|+e+\sqrt e\bigr).$$ The terms with a derivative of $\varphi$ have the flat tensor expression in <a href="#eq:lt-height-normal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-height-normal">[eq:lt-height-normal]</a>, with a coefficient error at most $C\gamma|P_{vh}|$. The curved horizontal identity is covariant, with $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}_SX$ and $\nabla^SX$ in place of the flat base operators. Its geometric error is bounded by the right side of <a href="#eq:lt-scaledvariation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-scaledvariation">[eq:lt-scaledvariation]</a> with the integrand multiplied by $|f|$. The additional flux error from the height gradient is bounded pointwise by $$\label{eq:lt-horizontal-height-error}
 C\gamma |Df||X|(\sqrt e+\sqrt\lambda).$$ In its coordinate form the divergence comparison also contributes an error bounded by $Cl\int|f||X|\,\mathop{}\!d\mu$; the coordinate changes in the $B$ term are included in the preceding geometric error. Under a further interior rescaling, retain the original horizontal and normal frames and the original $\gamma$ in <a href="#eq:lt-small-geometry" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-small-geometry">[eq:lt-small-geometry]</a>. Transform the displayed estimates by change of variables. The inherited physical connection remains $O(l)$; no improvement to $O(ls)$ is asserted on an off-center ball of coordinate radius $s$. In particular first-order flux errors have size at most $C\gamma(\sqrt e+\sqrt\lambda)$ times the relevant multiplier, whereas quadratic errors have size at most $C\gamma(e+\lambda)$. The factor $|v|$ in <a href="#eq:lt-normal-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-normal-error">[eq:lt-normal-error]</a> must be retained when estimating a normal test.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lt-heighttests"><summary>Proof of stmt-lt-heighttests</summary>

*Proof.* In a product Euclidean chart, differentiate the ambient fields $f(\zeta)X(y)$ and $\varphi(y)v(\zeta)$. Their horizontal and vertical blocks give <a href="#eq:lt-height-horizontal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-height-horizontal">[eq:lt-height-horizontal]</a> and <a href="#eq:lt-height-normal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-height-normal">[eq:lt-height-normal]</a>. Apply the first with $f=\Gamma$ and $X=(y-x)G_s$. It yields the first term of <a href="#eq:lt-height-gaussian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:lt-height-gaussian">[eq:lt-height-gaussian]</a> and the flux $s\int G_sD\Gamma\cdot P_{vh}u\,\mathop{}\!d\mu$. Apply the normal formula with $v=D\Gamma$ and $\varphi=G_s$; since $\nabla G_s=-G_su/s$, this flux is exactly $s^2\int G_sD^2\Gamma:P_{vv}\,\mathop{}\!d\mu$. This proves the identity and fixes both its sign and its factors of $s$.

We give the geometric error calculation.
